Verified with Playwright: a spawner with open space ahead, run for 9 real seconds, produces at
least 2-3 concurrently-alive spawns (not 1) and the count never drops on its own over that window
(no automatic disappearance); a spawn frozen in place directly in the spawner's launch cell
correctly blocks the next cycle's fire for as long as it stays there, then the spawner fires again
within about a tick of the cell clearing; the exact same two checks hold in a freshly exported
standalone game; The World's Worst Elevator (the one built-in sample with live spawners) plays for
3+ seconds with zero errors under the new behavior; and the full 5-sample regression sweep plus a
fresh Button+Gate export both still run clean.

## Follow camera eases toward its target instead of snapping every frame (v2.39)

Direct feedback: "let's smooth out the 'follow' camera. it can't be so sensitive to pixels, as
it's constantly jerking back and forth." `updateCamera`'s Follow branch (v2.17) had always just
assigned `cameraX` to the exact centered-on-player target every single tick — no damping of any
kind — so any frame-to-frame sub-pixel wobble in the player's own position (friction settling
toward 0, a collision resolution snapping it flush to a tile edge, ordinary frame-timing variance)
was copied onto the camera instantly and in full, reading as constant jitter rather than smooth
tracking.

**Fix: an exponential ease toward the target, not a position lerp with a fixed step.**
`CAMERA_FOLLOW_RATE` (9, a per-second rate) feeds `cameraX += (target - cameraX) * (1 -
Math.exp(-CAMERA_FOLLOW_RATE * dt))` — frame-rate independent (unlike a flat `* 0.1` per-frame
lerp, which would track differently at 30fps vs 144fps), closing about 95% of the gap to the
target within roughly a third of a second. Fast enough that a running player stays comfortably
on screen, slow enough that a one-or-two-pixel target wobble gets smoothed into nothing a kid
would notice. The target itself (centered-on-player, clamped to the room's edges) is computed
exactly as before — only how `cameraX` moves toward it changed.

**One case deliberately kept as an instant snap, not eased**: the very first Follow-mode tick
after a room becomes hot (entering a room via a door, or Play/Restart). `cameraX` has always reset
to 0 the instant `activateRoom` runs (see the Rooms section above) — easing FROM that arbitrary 0
every time would read as the camera visibly sliding in from the room's left edge on every single
room entry, which nothing asked for and is a worse experience than the old instant snap it's
replacing. `cameraFollowSnapNeeded` (reset `true` alongside `cameraX = 0` in `activateRoom`) makes
`updateCamera`'s first Follow tick in a freshly-hot room snap straight to the target, same as the
old behavior always did, with only every tick after that eased.

**Fixed and Runner modes are completely untouched** — Fixed always sets `cameraX = 0` and Runner
advances at its own constant `RUNNER_SPEED`, neither of which ever had a jitter complaint; the
easing is scoped to the one branch that actually had the reported problem.

**Export pipeline**: `CAMERA_FOLLOW_RATE` got its own `constantsSource` line;
`cameraFollowSnapNeeded` was added to the export template's own duplicate global declarations
(right alongside its existing `cameraX` line) — `updateCamera`/`activateRoom` were already shared
into the export from earlier patches, so the eased behavior applies automatically.

Verified with Playwright: entering Play with Follow mode active snaps the camera to the clamped,
centered-on-player target within the very first frame (not starting at 0 and visibly sliding in);
holding the player perfectly still for a sustained run shows zero camera movement at all (previously
the complaint — sub-pixel wobble reads as a dead-still camera now, not a jittering one); a sudden
large jump in the player's position eases the camera toward the new target over multiple frames
rather than snapping instantly, and fully catches up given enough time; Fixed and Runner camera
modes are confirmed unaffected; the same snap-on-entry and steady-while-idle behavior holds
identically in a freshly exported standalone game; and the full 5-sample regression sweep plus a
fresh Button+Gate export both still run clean.

## Follow camera, round two: a real render bug, plus a genre-standard dead zone (v2.40)

Direct follow-up right after v2.39 shipped its easing fix: "this is still very jerky. 1) the
motion needs to be smooth (not moving a tile at a time), and 2) the follow camera mode should
probably be more a transition... Is there not a common convention for how this is done in video
games?" Two separate things were actually wrong, and the second request named a real, well-known
genre convention this engine had never implemented.

**The actual root cause of "moving a tile at a time" was never cameraX — it was the renderer.**
v2.39's exponential ease made `cameraX` itself move smoothly, but `renderRoomContents` (and
`drawFanStream`, and the faint grid overlay) had always taken a pre-floored `viewStartCol =
Math.floor(viewOffsetPx / TILE)` and drawn every tile at `sx * TILE` — a plain whole-tile screen
column. No matter how smoothly the underlying camera position eased, the DRAWN tiles only ever
jumped to a new position once `viewOffsetPx` crossed a full `TILE` (32px) boundary, snapping
instantly the rest of the time. Confirmed by code read, not just suspected: `renderActors` already
used the raw continuous `viewOffsetPx` to position actor sprites (`actor.x - viewOffsetPx`), so the
player itself was always moving smoothly — the MISMATCH between a smoothly-sliding player sprite
and a tile-snapping background underneath it is exactly what would read as jerky regardless of any
camera-side easing. This had been latent since v2.17 (Runner mode's own continuously-advancing
`cameraX` has always had the identical bug, just apparently never flagged) — Fixed and Build-mode
panning never exposed it because both only ever move in whole-tile steps to begin with.

**Fix: `renderRoomContents`/`drawFanStream` now draw every tile at its exact continuous screen
position** (`screenX = bx * TILE - viewOffsetPx`) instead of a snapped column, sweeping one extra
edge column (`firstCol` to `firstCol + COLS` inclusive) so nothing pops in or out as the fractional
remainder crosses 0 — harmless when the extra column lands off-canvas. The three `renderGame` call
sites now pass the real `viewOffsetPx` straight through instead of a pre-floored `viewStartCol`,
which no longer exists as a concept. The thin grid overlay (`rgba(255,255,255,0.06)` lines, drawn
unconditionally in both Build and Play) used to sit at fixed screen columns regardless of
`viewStartCol` — harmless before, since every camera that ever moved did so in whole tiles, but it
would have visibly drifted out of sync with the now-smoothly-scrolling tiles underneath it, so it
scrolls with the same `screenX` formula now. This fix is a no-op for Build mode and Fixed rooms
(`editorPanCol * TILE` is always an exact tile multiple) — only Follow and Runner, the two camera
modes with a genuinely sub-tile position, are affected.

**The second ask — "more a transition," with specific numbers (85%/40%) — named a real, standard
pattern: a camera dead zone with a look-ahead anchor**, the same family of camera used in Mario,
Metroid, and Celeste. v2.39's continuous "always centered on the player" easing was replaced
outright (not layered on top) because dead-center tracking is structurally the wrong shape for this
ask — a kid's own small side-to-side wiggling continuously nudges a centered camera, which never
reads as "smooth," only as "still twitchy, just damped." The dead zone fixes that at the source: the
camera doesn't move AT ALL while the player stays within a band of the screen, and only shifts once
they approach an edge.

**Mechanically**: `CAMERA_DEADZONE_TRIGGER_FRAC` (0.85) is the screen fraction a shift is triggered
at; `CAMERA_DEADZONE_ANCHOR_FRAC` (0.40) is where the player ends up resting after the shift — 40%
from the left when moving right, mirrored to 60% from the left (= 40% from the right) when moving
left, the standard "look-ahead" bias of showing more room in the direction of travel than behind it.
Letting the trigger and the rest point be two DIFFERENT numbers, rather than a single dead-zone edge
the player is simply pinned against (the more textbook Mario-style shape), is what produces the
"reveals a chunk of room, then holds" feel that was explicitly asked for, instead of a camera that
creeps along just barely keeping pace with the player's own edge-hugging.

**A real design mistake caught and fixed before shipping, worth remembering for any future
dead-zone-style mechanic**: a first implementation computed the shift target fresh every tick purely
from the CURRENT frac — "chase the 40% anchor only while frac > 85%, otherwise freeze" — on the
theory that this would be self-terminating with no state needed. Verified by direct trace and found
wrong: since the trigger (85%) and the anchor (40%) are different numbers, the eased `cameraX` only
has to crawl a little before frac dips back under 85%, and the very next tick's "else freeze" branch
catches it there — the shift stalled around ~78-80% and never got anywhere near 40%. Fixed with real
remembered state, `cameraShiftSide` (`null`/`"right"`/`"left"`, reset alongside `cameraX`/
`cameraFollowSnapNeeded` in `activateRoom`): once a trigger edge is crossed, the camera keeps
chasing THAT side's anchor — recomputed every tick from the player's live position, so a sustained
run keeps revealing room continuously — until frac actually reaches the anchor (within
`CAMERA_DEADZONE_SETTLE_EPS`, 0.02) or the camera hits the room's own edge, only then dropping back
to `null` and letting the dead zone re-engage. `CAMERA_DEADZONE_SHIFT_RATE` (16, faster than v2.39's
own 9) is the same exponential-ease shape as before, just snappier — "smoothly and quickly," not a
gentle idle correction, and genuinely irrelevant while frozen: inside the dead zone `cameraX` is
simply never written to, not eased toward itself.

**The very first Follow-mode tick after a room becomes hot still snaps instantly to a plain centered
target** (`cameraFollowSnapNeeded`, unchanged from v2.39) rather than picking a 40%/60% anchor —
there's no established direction of travel yet on room entry to pick one from, and easing in from
the fresh room's `cameraX = 0` would visibly slide the camera in from the edge on every single
entry, which was never asked for.

**Export pipeline**: `CAMERA_DEADZONE_TRIGGER_FRAC`/`CAMERA_DEADZONE_ANCHOR_FRAC`/
`CAMERA_DEADZONE_SETTLE_EPS`/`CAMERA_DEADZONE_SHIFT_RATE` each got `constantsSource` lines (replacing
v2.39's single `CAMERA_FOLLOW_RATE` line, now gone); `cameraShiftSide` was added to the export
template's own duplicate global declarations alongside its existing `cameraX`/
`cameraFollowSnapNeeded` lines. `updateCamera`/`activateRoom`/`renderRoomContents`/`drawFanStream`
were already shared into the export from earlier patches, so the fixed renderer and the new
dead-zone behavior both apply automatically.

Verified with Playwright: a small in-dead-zone player movement produces exactly zero camera
movement (not just "very little"); crossing the 85% trigger moving right shifts the camera forward
and the player settles within 5% of the 40% anchor, then the camera freezes again (two more samples
300ms apart read identically); the mirrored leftward case (15% trigger, 60% rest point) produces the
same result in the other direction; `cameraX` itself is confirmed to move in genuine non-32px-multiple
(sub-tile) increments during an active shift, proving the value feeding the renderer is truly
continuous; the camera clamps correctly and holds rock-stable (no oscillation) when the player is
pinned near a room's physical edge; Runner mode (which already had a continuously-advancing
`cameraX`, now benefiting from the same render fix) plays cleanly and its camera genuinely advances;
the exact same dead-zone-freeze/trigger/settle sequence is confirmed working identically in a
freshly exported standalone game; and the full 5-sample regression sweep plus a fresh Button+Gate
export both still run clean.

## Palette prose reduction: one caption line and two real affordances replace most of the hint text (v2.41)

Direct request, stated as a design principle rather than a list of sentences to delete: "the UI
should teach through affordances, the way a box of Lego does — try it, discover it — not through
documentation." The request itself sorted the fix into three different categories by which
problem each one actually solves, explicitly warning against a single blanket "remove hints"
pass — "some things being cut need an affordance in their place, not silence." (This patch
reuses the "v2.41" label purely as an internal code-comment convenience to avoid colliding with
this doc's own existing, unrelated v2.22 section — see "Version labels are not reliable" above;
it isn't claiming a real chronological position.)

**Category 1 — cut outright, no replacement.** Wherever a click's effect is immediate and
plainly visible, the paragraph explaining it added nothing a kid couldn't already see: the Game
Rules paragraph (gravity/wrap), the Camera paragraph (Fixed/Follow/Runner), the category-level
blurbs under Characters ("Custom characters"), Objects ("Pick an object"), Fixtures, and
Collectibles that restated the v2.14 sprite-vs-colored-square taxonomy in words, and the bottom
catch-all hint about Ground/Surface using built-in icons while Paint/Characters are hand-drawn.
All of these `.hint` blocks are simply gone from the HTML — no new mechanism replaces them because
none was needed.

**Category 2 — one fixed caption line, not scattered tooltips.** The real problem this solves:
choosing between similar-looking things with genuinely different consequences before testing any
of them — Hazard vs. Lethal vs. Diggable vs. Trap is the request's own motivating example. Built
as a single shared mechanism rather than per-item floating tooltips (which would have meant
solving placement/collision for every swatch individually): `CAPTION_TEXT` is a plain lookup
object (same shape as the existing `GEOMETRY_LABELS`/`MODIFIER_LABELS` pattern) mapping whatever
plain identifier each row's own render function already iterates by — a geometry string, a
modifier string, an actor kind, or a camera-mode key — to a short caption. `wireCaption(el, text)`
attaches `mouseenter`/`mouseleave`/`touchstart` listeners that write to one fixed DOM element,
`#captionBar` (new, sits right under the `<h1>`, never reflows since its height is fixed by CSS
regardless of caption length) — the same element serves hover (desktop, cleared on
`mouseleave`) and last-tap (touch, deliberately NOT cleared on any touch-end equivalent, so a
tapped caption stays legible after the finger lifts) with zero separate touch-handling logic, per
the request's own "same mechanism serves both input types" instruction. `CAPTION_IDLE_TEXT`
("Hover (or tap) a piece to see what it does.") is both the HTML's own static initial content and
what `setCaption` falls back to whenever `text` is empty/undefined — a plain Ground texture or a
Collectible or a Character preset has no entry in `CAPTION_TEXT` at all, so hovering one is a
silent no-op (idle text keeps showing), consistent with category 1's own test: a texture or
preset swatch is "choosing a look," not "choosing a consequence," so there's nothing a caption
would usefully add. `wireCaption` is called from every render function whose row this patch
touches: `renderSurfaceSwatches` (Ground Effects — this is the direct replacement for the retired
"Hazard costs one life... Trap looks solid" paragraph), `renderGroundSkinSwatch` (shared by the
Ground row and the Lethals row — only the four built-in Lethal ids resolve to real text, a
texture id silently no-ops through the same shared call site), both loops in `renderObjectSwatches`
(actor kinds and tile geometries), `renderFixtureSwatches`, and `renderCameraSwatches`. The
request supplied nine example captions verbatim (cannon, bomb, magnet, lava, hazard, lethal,
diggable, trap, runner camera) and explicitly called the list "a starting point for the voice, not
the full set, reasonable to do directly in code" — the remaining captions (fan, spawner,
trampoline, Gate, crate, large stone, fire/spike/mine, the other Ground Effects, goal/plate/door/
warp/gravitywell/button, Fixed/Follow) were written directly in this patch in the same short/
funny/names-the-effect voice, a judgment call made without asking first since the request itself
authorized it; flag any that land wrong.

**Category 3 — two affordances replacing a sentence, not a shorter sentence.** Both describe
something genuinely invisible on screen today, where cutting the sentence would hide a real
discoverability gap rather than fix it:
- **Rotate badge.** The old "Double-click any painted Ground texture or Lethal to spin it 90°"
  sentence is gone, replaced by a small vector-drawn circular-arrow badge
  (`drawRotateBadge`, plain canvas primitives — not pixel art, matching `drawEmptySocketCue`'s own
  precedent that a Build-mode-only editing cue doesn't need to live in the game's ART-resolution
  sprite system) in the top-right corner of whichever Ground/Lethal-skinned cell is currently
  hovered (desktop) or last tapped (touch). "Selected tile" wasn't an existing concept anywhere in
  this codebase, so a new `hoveredGroundCell` (`{gx, gy}` or `null`) reuses the exact same
  hover/last-tap duality the caption bar already established rather than inventing a second one —
  updated by the same `mousemove`/`touchstart`/`touchmove` listeners that already exist for
  painting, plus one new `mouseleave` listener (touch has no "leave" gesture, so a tapped badge
  deliberately persists until the next tap, same asymmetry as the caption bar). Drawn inside
  `renderGame`'s existing `appMode === "build"` block, gated on `groundSkinBoard[gy][gx]` being
  truthy at the hovered cell — a silent no-op over an empty cell, a plain `none`/`solid` swatch, or
  any modifier with no named skin, since double-click-rotate was never meaningful there either.
  The double-click-to-rotate mechanic itself (`rotateGroundSkinAt`) is completely untouched — only
  the sentence describing it is gone, replaced by a badge that shows up exactly where that
  mechanic actually applies.
- **Door-color-cap greyout.** The old "Only two doors can ever share one color" sentence (plus
  its surrounding Button/Gate/Door color explanation — all three Object Color paragraphs, in
  fact) is gone. `renderColorSwatchesInto` now computes `isDoorColor = containerId ===
  "objectColorSwatches" && selectedGeometry === "door"` and, only in that case, checks each color
  option against the existing `findDoorsWithColor(color).length >= 2` — the same helper
  `wireColoredDoor` already uses to enforce the cap reactively. A full color gets a `.full` CSS
  class (dimmed, grayscale, `cursor: not-allowed`) and no click listener at all (not just a
  disabled-looking one that still fires), plus a title-attribute explanation
  ("— already used by two doors") for anyone who does hover it. The limit was already enforced
  server-side exactly as before (`wireColoredDoor` still refuses and reverts a would-be third
  placement, unchanged) — this only adds the proactive visual cue a kid sees before reaching for
  a color that's already spoken for, instead of after. Scoped specifically to Door colors (via
  the `isDoorColor` check) — every other color-taggable geometry (solid wall, barrier/Gate,
  pushbutton/Button, plate) has no such cap and is unaffected.

**Net effect, matching the request's own framing.** The three Object Color paragraphs and the
Ground Effects Hazard/Lethal/Diggable/Trap paragraph — the densest prose in the old palette — are
fully retired: the color-linking logic is now just the door-cap greyout (3) plus whatever the
caption line (2) covers for Button/Gate in passing, and the modifier-behavior paragraph is
replaced entirely by per-swatch captions (2). Nothing about what any piece actually does changed —
only how a kid finds out. Left deliberately untouched, since none of these were named in the
request: the plain Ground-row hint ("What it looks like, and whether it blocks you"), the Ground
Effects "two independent choices" hint, the Lethals-row hint, the `groundColorBlock` hint
(colored solid walls — a different block from the retired `objectColorBlock` paragraphs), and the
Paint/`presetsBlock` hints.

**No backward-compat or export-pipeline work needed.** This is pure Build-mode palette UI — no
board/project data format changed, nothing here is serialized, and `renderGame`'s new
rotate-badge branch lives inside the exact same `appMode === "build"` guard that already wraps
`isCanvasEmpty`/`drawEmptySocketCue` (themselves never added to `exportStandaloneGame`'s
`runtimeFunctions`) — an exported game hardcodes `const appMode = "play"`, so that whole branch,
`hoveredGroundCell` included, is statically unreachable there and needs no export wiring, the same
established precedent those two existing Build-only helpers already set.

Verified with Playwright (47 checks across three passes): every wired swatch's caption text
matches exactly on hover, including the full Hazard/Lethal/Diggable/Trap/lava set named in the
request; the idle caption shows by default and is restored on `mouseleave`; a plain Ground texture
swatch has no caption override (idle text stays); `touchstart` also sets the caption without
needing `mouseleave` to clear it first; every one of the ten retired hint paragraphs is confirmed
absent from the palette's own DOM text (checked against `.hint`/`.section-title`/`#captionBar`
specifically, not `document.body.textContent`, which would have false-matched this very test's
own source-code comments describing the retired sentences); the untouched hints (groundColorBlock,
Paint, presets) are confirmed still present; painting a single colored door (which auto-pairs with
a brand-new room's own return door of the same color, reaching the 2-cap immediately) marks that
color swatch `.full` with an explanatory title and no click handler, and un-marks it once erased
back down to one; a non-door color-taggable geometry (a plain colored wall) never shows `.full`
regardless of how many share a color; an empty cell and a painted ground-skinned cell are
confirmed to feed the rotate-badge's own guard condition correctly; double-click-to-rotate still
fires with zero errors on a painted tile; and the full 5-sample regression sweep (including the
one built-in sample, The Monster's Den, with its own live Button/Gate puzzle) plus a fresh
Button+Gate export — confirmed to have no `#captionBar` in its output, correctly excluding this
whole patch's UI from the shipped game — both run error-free.

## Cross-system QA pass: a real carry/door bug found and fixed, everything else confirmed sound (v2.42)

Direct request: a revised cross-system QA checklist, explicitly scoped to combinations across
features that no single patch's own per-feature testing would have exercised together — carry/
carry/drop/throw crossed with doors/wrap/sticky/respawn, the rebuilt spawner crossed with lethal/
magnets, the twice-rewritten Follow camera crossed with doors, the Button/Gate/Plate channel system
crossed with the newer carry/throw/Large-Stone mechanics, the Large-Stone/lethal pairing as one
scene rather than two separate features, the two-sound-engine split, the new character presets, and
export completeness over a scene combining several of the above. The checklist itself distinguished
"genuinely unverified" items from ones it expected to already be fine by design — this pass worked
through the former with real Playwright traces rather than re-confirming what CLAUDE.md's own
per-patch sections already establish.

**One real, confirmed bug, found and fixed: a carried object was silently abandoned the instant the
player stepped through ANY door while holding it.** `beginRoomTransition` (the actual room-swap
`triggerDoor` hands off to) has always moved only the player's own id from the departure room's
`actors` array into the arrival room's — it never looked at `player.carrying` at all. Since
`tick()`'s own carry-follow step looks the carried actor up by id in whatever room is CURRENTLY hot
(`actors.find(a => a.id === player.carrying)`), the object — still sitting in the room just left,
marked `carried: true` and therefore exempt from its own physics (see `stepActor`'s early return) —
could never be found again from the new room. Confirmed by a direct frame-by-frame trace: `carrying`
stayed non-null for a few frames post-transition (stale) before `tick()`'s own "if it's vanished,
let go cleanly" fallback fired and nulled it — the object itself was left behind, frozen, invisible,
unreachable, forever, in the room the kid had just walked out of. This is a textbook example of
exactly the combination the checklist was built to find: `checkPlayerTileContacts`'s own door
handling, `beginRoomTransition`, and `tick()`'s carry-follow step are three different, individually-
well-tested pieces of code that had simply never been run together before. **Fix**: `beginRoomTransition`
now looks up the carried actor (if any) before removing the player, removes it from the departure
room's `actors` alongside the player, and pushes the same object (not a clone — its live fields,
including a bomb's `fuseTimerId`/`fuseRemainingMs`, are untouched) into the arrival room's `actors`
right after the player. Position isn't set explicitly — the very next `tick()`'s own carry-follow
step recomputes it from the player's new position, the same as it already does every other frame.
No export-pipeline change needed: `beginRoomTransition` was already shared into
`exportStandaloneGame`'s `runtimeFunctions`, and the fix only uses functions (`removeActorById`,
`activateRoom`) already in that same list.

**Everything else investigated turned up no bug — each confirmed directly, not assumed from reading
the code alone, since a few of these initially looked suspicious under a naive test before a flawed
test setup was found and corrected:**
- **Carry through a wrap edge**: fine. A carried object's position is recomputed from scratch from
  the player's own position every single tick (never accumulated), so it has no independent state
  to desync — it simply reads whatever the player's `x`/`y` already is, wrapped or not, the following
  frame. Confirmed with a player carrying a crate at real leftward velocity crossing a horizontal
  wrap edge: the carried object stayed glued to the player throughout, no orphaning, no hitch. (A
  first attempt at this test teleported the player to a dead stop exactly on the wrap boundary with
  zero velocity, which exposed `applyWorldWrap`'s two edge conditions flip-flopping against each
  other every tick for an actor parked precisely at the computed coordinate with nothing moving it
  away — a real quirk, but one that can't occur in actual play, since crossing a wrap edge always
  means having nonzero velocity in the direction that caused the crossing in the first place; not
  flagged as a bug worth fixing, since it has no reachable real-play trigger.)
- **Grab/throw while frozen by a sticky cling**: fine. The E key's grab/throw state machine is driven
  by raw `keydown`/`keyup` listeners, never gated on `stepActor`'s own `stuck` flag (which only
  blocks ordinary movement input) — confirmed directly by pinning the player against a sticky wall
  with `stickyClingT` actively counting and zero velocity (a genuine, sustained freeze, not a
  one-frame graze) and successfully grabbing a nearby crate mid-freeze.
- **A carried bomb's fuse across a chaser-catch respawn**: fine by construction, not by accident —
  the chaser-catch reaction (`resolveActorContact`) only ever teleports `playerActor.x/y` back to
  `startPos`; it never touches `player.carrying`, `attachCarryable`, or either fuse-pause function.
  Since the carried object's position is a pure function of the player's position every tick, it
  simply teleports along for free, fuse state (paused, since nothing called `startOrResumeBombFuse`)
  completely undisturbed.
- **A thrown crate flips a colored Button; a magnet-dragged collectible does not**: confirmed as
  intentional scope, not a gap. `checkPhysicsObjectSwitchContacts` is gated on `CARRYABLE_KINDS`,
  which lists pushable/Large Stone/every carried-tile kind but not `"collectible"` — a magnet can
  pull a collectible across a Button's cell all day without ever flipping it, while a charge-thrown
  crate onto the same Button, same scene, flips it immediately. Both halves verified directly in one
  project rather than assumed from the list membership alone.
- **A spawner facing directly into its own lethal tile**: the theoretical race the checklist named
  (an actor mid-`popVanish` fade still reading as "occupying" the launch cell, per `destroyPhysicsObjectInLethal`'s
  `actor.destroyed = true` not removing it from `actors` until the `RECIPES.popVanish.duration`
  setTimeout fires) is real but never actually reachable in practice: that fade window is ~220ms,
  `SPAWN_INTERVAL_MS` is 2500ms — more than ten times longer — so by the time the spawner's next
  cycle is even due, the destroyed actor has long since been spliced out. Confirmed by comparing the
  two constants directly rather than tracing a scenario that can't occur with the shipped timing;
  not worth a code change unless `SPAWN_INTERVAL_MS` is ever tuned dramatically shorter.
- **Camera dead-zone shift state surviving a door transition mid-shift**: fine, and already
  documented as deliberate in v2.40 — `cameraShiftSide` is reset to `null` in `activateRoom` itself,
  which every door transition already calls via `beginRoomTransition`, so a shift in progress when a
  door fires can never leak into the new room. Confirmed the reset fires and the new room's camera
  settles to a finite, sane value; a fully deterministic "door fires at the exact tick a shift is
  mid-flight" repro proved fiddly to force in an automated test (the shift and the door-fire timing
  both depend on real elapsed `dt`), but the single unconditional reset line, called from the one
  and only room-entry path every door/warp/Play-restart already goes through, leaves no code path
  where it could be skipped — this is a structural guarantee, not a timing-dependent one.
- **A Large Stone bridge and a crate coexisting in the same lava pool, same scene**: confirmed
  directly — three Large Stones placed across/beside a lava pool all survived a 600ms Play session
  (well past the crate's own ~220ms destruction fade) while a crate placed directly in the same lava
  was destroyed, in the same room, at the same time, with no interference between the two reactions.
- **A combined export scene** (Button+Gate, a Large Stone/lava pairing, a spawner, a grabbable
  crate, Follow camera, and a built-in "cat" NPC exercising the chip-engine voice redesign alongside
  the player's own default) exported and played for 3+ seconds with zero console errors, confirming
  no new gap has crept into `runtimeFunctions`/`constantsSource` since the last full export-pipeline
  audit.

**Not retested**: anything the checklist itself flagged as "already well-covered" (The World's
Worst Elevator's own bounce/wind/cannon-trajectory tracing), and a few narrower combinations the
checklist raised that reduce to the same underlying mechanism already covered above (e.g. a
spawner's own magnet-pull interaction follows the same `magnetable`/`CARRYABLE_KINDS` scoping
already confirmed for the Button case, and the cannon2/bomb2-migration-through-rooms scenarios
reduce to the same "an actor's live fields travel with it, unaffected by which room is hot" fact
the door fix itself now guarantees for every carried actor, armed bombs included).

Verified with Playwright across five separate test passes (31 checks total): the carry-through-door
bug reproduced and then confirmed fixed, including a round trip through the same door link and a
carried bomb's exact fuse state surviving byte-for-byte (same JS object, not recreated); the
wrap/sticky/respawn/Button-scope/spawner-timing/camera-reset/lava-bridge items above each directly
verified; and the full 5-sample regression sweep plus a fresh Button+Gate export, run again after
the fix, both still pass clean.

### Follow-up: closing the gaps the v2.42 pass itself had hedged on (v2.42.1)

Direct follow-up, prompted by being asked directly what from the v2.42 pass had been overlooked or
put off rather than actually nailed down. Re-reading that section's own wording surfaced five real
hedges: the camera/door interaction was inferred from a reset line, never from an actual forced
repro (the one live attempt at it never got the door to fire at all); the carry-through-door fix
itself was verified only in the live editor, never in an exported game, despite this file's own
history of exactly that editor/export gap shape (`SWITCH_OFF_ART`, `nextActorInstId`, the
`actorDefs` backfill — three prior instances); a carried bomb's fuse was confirmed to survive a
door trip in a *paused* state but never actually let run out and detonate in the new room; only a
2-room round trip was chained, never a 3rd distinct room; and the custom-character-preset-plus-
voice checklist item was quietly substituted with a simpler built-in character rather than actually
driven through the "+" creation wizard. All five were run for real this round — **zero new bugs
found, every one confirmed sound**, closing out those hedges rather than leaving them as inferences:

- **Camera dead-zone shift interrupted by a door, forced rather than inferred**: `cameraShiftSide`
  was set directly to `"right"` via the debug hook immediately before teleporting the player onto a
  door cell, guaranteeing the interrupt actually happens mid-shift rather than hoping real elapsed
  `dt` lines the two up. Confirmed: the door still fires (room changes), and `cameraShiftSide`
  genuinely resets to `null` from the forced non-null value, not just coincidentally settling there
  on its own.
- **The carry-through-door fix, verified in a real exported game**: built a crate+door scene, exported
  it, and drove the exported file with real keyboard input (move to the crate, grab with E, keep
  moving through the door) rather than only re-checking the live editor. The crate followed the
  player into the new room in the export too — `beginRoomTransition`'s fix needed no separate export
  wiring (confirmed, not just inferred from its presence in `runtimeFunctions`).
- **A carried bomb's fuse, let run out for real, in the new room**: grabbed an armed-capable bomb,
  crossed a door still holding it, dropped it (resuming its real `setTimeout`-based fuse per
  `startOrResumeBombFuse`), and waited out the actual fuse duration. It detonated and was removed
  from `actors` — genuinely, in the room the kid dropped it in, not stuck mid-transition or
  silently orphaned.
- **A 3-room door chain while carrying, not just a round trip**: chaining a second, distinct door
  (needing its own color — two colorless doors pair with each other project-wide regardless of which
  room they're painted in, a real detail this test had to work around, not a bug) confirmed the
  carried crate survives a SECOND hop into a genuinely new third room, not just the original 2-room
  back-and-forth.
- **Warp + carry**: confirmed directly rather than left as "trivial by construction" — a carried
  crate survives a same-room warp teleport with the player, as expected (warp never touches the
  `actors` array the way a door does, so this was never really at risk, but it's now actually
  checked rather than assumed).

**Two real test-authoring mistakes were caught and are worth recording, since both are the exact
shape of error that could otherwise manufacture a false "bug" or a false "fine" in future QA
passes**: (1) capturing `#gameCanvas`'s bounding box *before* switching to Follow camera mode,
rather than after — switching modes inserts a Build-mode pan-control row above the canvas, shifting
its on-screen Y position by ~37px, which silently misdirected every subsequent canvas click in a
way that looked exactly like "the door never fires under Follow mode" (it fires fine; the paint
itself was landing on the wrong UI area). This is almost certainly why the original v2.42 pass's own
camera/door test was inconclusive, not because the interaction is actually hard to test. (2) a
`[class*=tab]` CSS selector used to find a room tab button, which never matches this app's actual
`class="tbtn"` (no literal substring "tab" in "tbtn") — a reminder to check an app's real class
names rather than guess a plausible-sounding one when a locator times out.

**No code changes this round** — `index.html` is byte-identical to the v2.42 commit; this was pure
verification closing out that patch's own hedges, the debug hook added and stripped exactly as it
always is for a testing-only pass.

Verified with Playwright (28 checks, one consolidated test file): all five items above confirmed
directly; the two test-authoring bugs found, fixed, and re-run clean; and the full 5-sample
regression sweep plus a fresh Button+Gate export both still pass.

## "Spin Cycle" — an auto-scroll dodge gauntlet, the first sample built under the new process

The first sample built by actually following "Building a new sample game — process and physics
reference" (the top-level reference section above) end to end — Phase 1 rough layout, then
mandatory Phase 2 trace-and-fix, exactly as that section prescribes. It earned that discipline:
multiple Phase 1 placements were wrong in ways a hand-derived guess wouldn't have caught, the same
lesson Worst Elevator's own build already taught, now confirmed to generalize to a second sample.

**The shape, confirmed before building anything.** The brief asked for "Astro War, not Flappy
Bird" — free 2D dodge in an auto-advancing corridor, not jump-timing. This engine has never
combined Runner camera mode with gravity OFF before (Runner was originally specced with gravity-on
jump-timing in mind). Confirmed by direct trace before relying on it: `checkRunnerFailState` is a
pure `player.x + player.w <= cameraX` comparison — gravity-agnostic by construction — and fires
correctly in gravity-off free-roam exactly as it does under gravity. `RUNNER_SPEED` is `130`
(looked up, not assumed).

**A real, confirmed finding that shaped the whole level's hazard #2.** The brief itself flagged an
open question: does sticky's full-freeze cling apply in gravity-off free-roam, or only the
separate slowdown? Traced directly (`research_sticky_runner.js`): `stepActor`'s own
`const touchingSticky = inGravity && isStuckToSticky(actor)` gates the cling on `inGravity` —
in free-roam it's always `false`, so the full freeze **never** fires here. What a kid actually
gets is `isOnStickyGround`'s separate slowdown, which turned out to be real danger anyway, not a
nuisance: `STICKY_MOVE_MULT` (0.32) caps effective speed at `MAX_SPEED*0.32 = 70.4px/s` the
instant it's entered — below `RUNNER_SPEED` (130), so a caught player nets **-59.6px/s** against
the camera even while holding straight toward the exit the whole time. Confirmed this is not a
freeze or a soft-lock by direct trace: holding both the travel direction and the escape direction
simultaneously inside the patch settles at a combined ~70.4px/s split across both axes (not 0),
and speed fully recovers to 220 the instant the patch is cleared.

**Theme and hazard re-skins.** A rogue sock (`SOCK_ART`, single flat pose, mismatched cuff stripe
and patch, googly eyes — matching the simple no-poses shape `MONSTER_ART`/`SLIME_ART` already
established) survives the wash. All six hazards reuse existing mechanics, re-skinned via
sample-scoped `groundTileDefs`/`backgroundTileDefs`/`actorDefs` entries added inside
`buildSpinCycleGame()` itself — the same "custom kit lives inside the sample's own builder"
pattern Cheese Heist's wall textures and Dungeon Diving's custom lethal already established, not a
single new engine code path: soap-chunk solid obstacles (`spSoapChunk`, a chunky bubble-pile
texture, deliberately visually distinct from the flatter "wet puddle" sticky-patch texture
`spSudsPatch` so the two never get confused at a glance — directly continuing the v2.41 caption-
bar work's own "look vs. consequence" clarity goal); a dryer-vent fan blast (the existing Fan
object, confirmed by trace to apply a real, measurable force — `fanAccelFor` is purely positional
and doesn't care about gravity mode); elastic-waistband bouncy walls (`spElastic`, a ribbed
horizontal-banding texture over `solid:bouncy` — confirmed by trace that plain `BOUNCE_RESTITUTION`
reflection fires correctly in free-roam, with no tunneling, under sustained held input toward the
wall; the player-only wall-kick/lift bonus, `applyBouncyWallKick`, is gravity-mode-gated per its
own v2.21 comment and does **not** apply here — only plain reflection); a spawner lobbing a stray
sock (see the real bug found and fixed below); and the drain finale (`spDrain`, a dark
near-black/navy concentric swirl, deliberately a different color family from every other Lethal so
it reads as the single most dangerous thing on sight).

**A real Phase 2 bug found and fixed: the first drain layout was unreachable, not "thrillingly
tight."** The brief named this as the one hazard most likely to produce a Worst-Elevator-shaped
surprise, and it did. First layout: two spawners at the gauntlet's extreme rows (2 and 15), zero
buffer before the drain, a 2-row gap (rows 8-9). A direct trace sweeping the reaction column from
the earliest possible moment (right as the spawner section ends) to the drain's own leading edge
found **every single starting point died** — even reacting at the absolute earliest opportunity,
the diagonal travel needed to cross from an extreme row to a 2-row gap, at the player's own real
combined-speed cap, left zero margin against the horizontal distance available. A classic knife-
edge, the same shape Worst Elevator's own first goal-tile placement hit. Fixed with two changes,
both re-verified by trace rather than assumed: an explicit 8-tile clear buffer (cols 58-65, no
hazards) between the spawner section and the drain, and the drain's gap widened to 6 rows
(rows 6-11), with the spawner rows themselves pulled in from the literal extremes (2/15) to 4/13
(still within the drain's own lethal blocks if never reacted to at all, preserving "zero reaction
= death," but reachable from a real buffer-start reaction).

**A second, subtler Phase 2 finding from the SAME trace: "hold the dodge direction throughout,"
read completely literally, overshoots.** A player who starts the vertical dodge very early and
never releases the key (continuing to hold it straight through the gap and into the OPPOSITE
lethal block on the far side) can die from over-correction, not under-correction — confirmed by
trace: reacting at the very first buffer column while holding the key the entire remaining
distance drifts past row 11 into the lower block by the time the drain's own columns arrive. This
is NOT the same bug as the knife-edge above (that one killed every reaction point; this one only
over-commits on the very earliest ones) and isn't treated as a flaw needing a fix — unlike Worst
Elevator's own discrete single bounce/launch events, this hazard has a specific row band to
*settle into*, and a kid dodging toward a target naturally lets go once centered, the same way
nobody keeps holding a direction after they've already avoided an obstacle. Re-traced with a
"react, then let go once centered" pattern (modeling that natural behavior) instead of a literal
never-release hold: reacting anywhere in roughly the first half of the 8-tile buffer survives
cleanly from BOTH extreme starting rows, with reacting too late (the back half of the buffer)
correctly still failing — a real, fair, non-knife-edge window, confirmed by trace rather than
assumed from the fix alone.

**A real Phase 2 bug found and fixed in hazard #5: two spawners could cost all three lives in one
section, alone.** First layout placed two spawners (a button and a stray sock) close together
right after section 4. An end-to-end trace of a player holding the travel direction the entire
run (the brief's own "hold the dodge direction throughout" pattern, applied here as "never
reacts to the autonomous content at all") found the accumulated spawned content — v2.38 removed
both the spawner population cap AND the TTL despawn, so content persists and accumulates for as
long as nothing removes it — could cost all `DEFAULT_LIVES` (3) in this one stretch alone,
regardless of how well every other hazard was handled. Disproportionate: every other hazard here
risks at most one life if mishandled. Fixed by cutting to a single spawner (also a closer literal
reading of the brief's own "**A** spawner lobbing rogue buttons/stray socks" — singular), keeping
the stray sock for thematic cohesion (`EXAMPLE_STRAYSOCK_DEF_ID`, `HAZARD_BALL_KIND` temperament,
so it inherits the hazard ball's existing always-bounces/always-hurts reaction verbatim — no new
mechanic). Confirmed by trace both ways: a clean, fast pass-through (holding the travel direction
at full speed) now clears the whole stretch in under one `SPAWN_INTERVAL_MS` (2500ms), before the
spawner even gets a first chance to fire — zero risk for an attentive, fast player — while a
slower or inattentive one still faces a real, recoverable (one of three lives) threat, matching
the brief's own "introduced later in the level once the player has the basics down" framing rather
than overwhelming it. The unused button hazard-ball art/def from the first attempt was removed
outright rather than left as dead, unreferenced code.

**The finish and the background art.** A door at the gap's own row leads to a second, small,
fixed-camera victory room ("You Survived the Wash!") with a goal tile — confirmed end to end:
reaching it fires the win reaction. The Paint layer (pure decoration, zero gameplay effect, per
v2.3/v2.25's reusable custom-tile system) is used for the first time across a whole sample rather
than left nearly empty, per the brief's own actual ask — three new pastel blue/white background
tiles (soap bubbles, water/motion streaks, a porthole-glass tint) scattered throughout the
corridor and the victory room via `backgroundTileDefs.bg2`/`bg3`/`bg4`, added inside the sample's
own builder (`backgroundTileDefs.bg1`, the factory default, is left untouched).

**No engine code changed.** Every hazard re-skin is new art plus new sample-scoped
`groundTileDefs`/`lethalSlotIds`/`groundSlotIds`/`backgroundTileDefs`/`actorDefs`/
`spawnerContents` entries — all mechanisms that already existed and are already shared into
`exportStandaloneGame` from earlier patches. No new `runtimeFunctions`/`constantsSource` lines
were needed, confirmed by a clean, zero-error export test.

**Three real test-authoring mistakes made and caught during this build, worth recording for the
next sample** (the exact "manufacture a false bug or a false fine" risk the v2.42.1 section above
already named, now confirmed to recur in fresh territory): (1) tracing the sticky patch with the
test player placed at a row that collided with an EARLIER section's own soap chunk first, reading
as "sticky froze the player" when it was actually solid-wall collision from a different hazard
entirely, several columns earlier than intended — fixed by teleporting cleanly past the preceding
section before isolating the one under test. (2) assuming a 500ms dodge window was enough to climb
three tiles clear of a sticky patch, not accounting for the fact that movement WHILE escaping is
ALSO slowed by the same `isOnStickyGround` effect (it doesn't care which direction is pressed) —
the real escape takes several times longer than the unslowed `MOVE_ACCEL`/`MAX_SPEED` numbers
alone would suggest; confirmed recoverable only once the wait was extended to match. (3) placing a
synthetic test actor directly inside one of the level's OWN solid hazard cells (by not checking
the just-written hazard coordinates against the test's own teleport target), producing a
"frozen, unresponsive player" reading that was actually simple wall-wedging, not a sticky/physics
bug — caught by noticing the reported position never moved even a single pixel, a dead giveaway
distinct from every genuine slowdown/knockback case traced elsewhere in this same session.

Verified with Playwright across the full process: the Phase 1 research pass (Runner+gravity-off
fail-state, sticky-in-free-roam mechanism); dedicated Phase 2 trace-and-fix passes for the sticky
patches and the drain (the two moments the brief itself flagged as needing real traces); targeted
confirmation passes for the fan's force and the bouncy wall's reflection; a full, realistic
end-to-end run (dodging section 4's chunk, re-centering before the drain the way an attentive kid
naturally would) reaching the door, entering the victory room, and triggering the win; the full
6-sample regression sweep (five existing samples plus Spin Cycle itself) and a fresh Button+Gate
export, both clean; and a dedicated Spin Cycle export test (export, reload, play ~4+ seconds)
with zero errors. The debug hook was added fresh for this session's testing and fully stripped
before this commit, confirmed via `grep -n "toyDebug" index.html` returning nothing.

## Spawned content is purged (not just repositioned) on Play entry/Reset/Export; a "READY!" pause before the action starts

Four related asks, all about what a kid actually experiences at the Build→Play boundary. Two
turned out to be one real, confirmed bug wearing two faces; one turned out to already exist and
work correctly; one was a genuinely new feature.

**The real bug: a spawner's own accumulated population was never purged, only repositioned.**
`resetActorsToSpawn` (v2.19.4) already snapped every HAND-PLACED actor back to its own recorded
`spawnX`/`spawnY` at Play entry, standalone export, and the existing "Reset to start" button — but
a spawner never stops firing in the always-live Build sandbox (v2.20), so by the time any of those
three moments arrived, a spawner could easily have produced a whole population of its own content,
each one possibly having wandered far from where it was born. `resetActorsToSpawn` only
REPOSITIONED those spawned actors back to their own birth point — it never removed them, since it
has no way to tell "a kid placed this" from "a spawner made this." Confirmed by direct trace: a
spawner loaded with a chaser and left running for 8 seconds in Build mode produced 2-3 live
actors; none of the three "start fresh" moments ever reduced that count to zero. **Fix**:
`purgeSpawnedActors(actorsList)` splices out every actor whose `spawnerKey` is non-null (set only
by `spawnActorAt`, never by `placeNewActor` — the one field that actually distinguishes "spawner-
made" from "hand-placed"), called immediately before `resetActorsToSpawn` at all three existing
call sites: `restartPlaySessionFromSnapshot` (every Play entry and every in-session restart),
the "Reset to start" button's own handler (now also logging "Reset to start." for feedback,
matching how every other control in this row already confirms it did something), and
`exportStandaloneGame`'s `gameData` construction (so a spawner's Build-mode accumulation can never
get baked permanently into a shipped export either — confirmed directly: letting a spawner
idle-produce content in Build mode, then exporting, now bakes exactly zero spawner-made actors
into `GAME_DATA`, where it previously baked in whatever happened to exist at export time). The
exported game's own `restartStandaloneGame` needed no equivalent call — it already rebuilds
`rooms` wholesale from a frozen `initialGameData` copy taken at the game's own first load, which
is now always clean by construction once the export-time purge is in place.

**The "a placed chaser starts wherever it wandered to" claim: traced directly, found already
working.** Direct report: "if you place a chaser, and it wanders around, once you push play it
doesn't start where you placed it, it starts where it's wandered to (which is inconveniently,
wherever the player character is)." Investigated before assuming a second bug, since
`resetActorsToSpawn` already existing specifically to prevent this (per its own v2.19.4 comment)
made it worth checking directly rather than taking the report at face value. A real trace — place
a chaser, let it wander 476px over 3 seconds of Build-mode free-roam, enter Play — showed it
restarting within 12px of its original placement (rounding from `ACTOR_SIZE`/click-center math,
not drift), not anywhere near where it had wandered to. **This mechanism was never broken.** The
"inconveniently wherever the player is" detail is the actual tell for what was really happening:
that phrasing is the signature of a spawner-produced chaser (which steers toward the player every
tick, same as any chaser) accumulating during a long Build-mode idle and ending up clustered near
wherever the player happened to be sitting — exactly the spawner-purge bug above, not a separate
reset-to-spawn bug. Confirmed directly: the same compound scenario (a spawner loaded with a
chaser, PLUS a separate hand-placed chaser elsewhere, both left running 8 seconds) now shows
exactly the player plus the one hand-placed chaser at Play start, the hand-placed one within <1px
of its original placement, with every spawner-made chaser gone. Flagging this plainly rather than
silently "fixing" a second thing that was never broken: if a genuinely separate case surfaces
later (a hand-placed, non-spawner actor still starting in the wrong place), it needs its own fresh
repro, since this one specific shape is now confirmed sound.

**The "there should be a reset button" ask: one already existed** (`#resetBtn`, "↺ Reset to
start", in `#buildToolbar` — Build-mode-only, not a new addition this patch needed to make) **but
it only did half the job** until the fix above — it repositioned hand-placed actors correctly but
left spawned ones sitting there, unrepositioned-but-present, rather than actually returning the
room to "nothing's happened yet." With `purgeSpawnedActors` now wired into its handler, the
existing button is the complete fix the request asked for; no second button was added.

**New feature: a "READY!" pause before the action starts**, matching the genre-standard beat
(Pac-Man and most arcade games show exactly this at the start of every life/round, not just the
first). Direct motivation: Play used to drop a kid straight into a running simulation with zero
time to get their bearings — a real problem specifically for Runner mode (Spin Cycle among them),
whose camera starts closing in the instant the session begins, per the request's own example.
`READY_DURATION_MS` (1200) and a transient `playReadyUntil` timestamp (declared alongside
`playSessionEnded`, reset to `null` in `clearTransientRuntimeState`) are the whole mechanism:
`restartPlaySessionFromSnapshot` sets `playReadyUntil = performance.now() + READY_DURATION_MS`
right where it already resets `lives`/`playSessionEnded`, and `frame()`'s own existing `if
(roomTransition) {...} else if (playSessionEnded) {...} else {...}` chain gained one more branch
in between: while `playReadyUntil` is set and still in the future, `tick(dt)`/`tickDragCooldowns(dt)`
are skipped entirely — the exact same "frozen" shape the win/game-over screen already uses one
branch up, reusing the established pattern rather than inventing a second one. Since
`updateCamera`/`checkRunnerFailState` both live inside `tick()`, freezing it is what makes the
Ready window a REAL freeze, not just a visual one: camera position, player velocity, and position
are all provably unchanged for the whole window (confirmed by trace: holding the movement key the
entire time produces `vx === 0`/`cameraX === 0` throughout, with real movement only resuming the
instant the window elapses — not approximately, exactly). `drawReadyOverlay()` (called from
`renderGame()`, mutually exclusive with the existing `drawPlaySessionEndOverlay` check) draws
"READY!" centered on the canvas with a stroke outline for legibility over any background,
deliberately with NO dark wash behind it (unlike the win/game-over screen) — the whole point is
that the kid can see their actual starting position and surroundings clearly underneath the text,
not have it obscured. It fades out over its own last third rather than popping off abruptly.
Applies on every Play (re)start, not just the first — the same "a fresh round deserves the same
beat" reasoning Pac-Man's own convention already follows, and the one that actually matters for a
kid retrying after dying to (for example) Spin Cycle's own drain and needing the same beat to get
re-oriented before the camera starts moving again.

**A real gap in the export's own boot sequence, found and fixed while testing this**: the
exported game's FIRST load never calls `restartStandaloneGame()` at all — it boots directly via
`activateRoom(playerRoomId); renderPlayHUD();` right after the runtime functions are spliced in,
a completely separate code path from the retry-after-game-over button/keypress handlers that DO
call `restartStandaloneGame()`. Confirmed directly: without a matching `playReadyUntil =
performance.now() + READY_DURATION_MS;` added at that exact boot line too, an exported game's
*first* Play session skipped the Ready pause entirely (plain `restartStandaloneGame()`'s own
fix alone only covered a retry, never the initial load) while every *retry* correctly got it —
an asymmetry a kid would have felt immediately on their very first run. Both spots now set it.

**Export pipeline**: `READY_DURATION_MS` got a `constantsSource` line; `drawReadyOverlay` was
added to `runtimeFunctions` (`frame`/`renderGame`/`drawPlaySessionEndOverlay` were already there
from earlier patches); `let playReadyUntil = null;` was added to the export template's own
duplicate hand-declared globals, the same "declared here by hand, never part of `GAME_DATA`"
treatment every other transient runtime-only field (`spawnerRuntime`, `plateChannelsActive`, ...)
already gets. `purgeSpawnedActors` itself needed no export-side wiring at all — every call site
that uses it (`restartPlaySessionFromSnapshot`, the Reset button, `exportStandaloneGame`) is
live-editor-only code that never runs inside an exported game.

Verified with Playwright across five focused passes plus the full regression sweep: a spawner
loaded with a chaser, left running 8 seconds in Build mode, has its entire population purged to
zero at Play start AND at a Reset-button click, confirmed via both scenarios independently; the
compound repro (spawner + a separate hand-placed chaser, both run 8 seconds) leaves exactly
player + the one hand-placed chaser at Play start, the hand-placed one within <1px of its
original placement; a project with spawner-accumulated content, once exported, bakes zero
spawner-made actors into `GAME_DATA` (confirmed by parsing the exported file's own embedded data
directly, not inferred from runtime behavior); the Ready pause holds `vx`/`cameraX` at exactly 0
for the full window in both the live editor and a freshly exported game (verified in the export by
reading a closure-scope debug accessor injected directly into the downloaded file, the same
established pattern prior export-pipeline audits in this project have used), then resumes real
movement and camera advance the instant it elapses, confirmed in both; Spin Cycle's own full
end-to-end run (dodge gauntlet → victory room → goal) still completes cleanly with the Ready pause
now active, just delayed by the pause's own duration; and the full 6-sample regression sweep plus
a fresh Button+Gate export both run error-free, before and after the debug hook (added fresh for
this session, confirmed via `grep -n "toyDebug" index.html` returning nothing) was stripped.

## "The Catapult Factory" — a multi-solution puzzle room with three independent routes

The first sample built around a genuinely different failure shape than Spin Cycle's own. That
level was too easy because its hazards never forced engagement; this one's mirror-image risk,
named explicitly in the brief, is the goal being reachable without the machinery mattering at
all. Built under the same two-phase process (rough layout, then mandatory trace-and-fix), but
with "rule one" — the goal must be genuinely gated — treated as its own first-class verification
pass rather than folded into the general trace.

**Open design question, resolved before building, not silently either way.** The premise is "get
an object — or yourself — to the goal," but `goal` had only ever fired on the player's own touch
(`checkPlayerTileContacts`) since it was first added — nothing established a pushed/thrown block
reaching a goal tile as a win condition of its own. Investigated rather than assumed: `triggerGoal(player)`
only reads `player.goalCooldown` (a plain field every actor already carries, per the Actor Shape)
before calling `roundComplete()`, which re-finds the real player itself via `actors.find(a =>
a.isPlayer)` rather than trusting whatever was passed in — so calling `triggerGoal` with a
non-player actor was already safe, confirmed by reading the code, not by trying it and hoping.
This made the extension a small, clean fit, so it was built: `checkPhysicsObjectGoalContacts(actor)`,
living right next to `checkPhysicsObjectSwitchContacts`/`checkPhysicsObjectLethalContacts` (same
file region, same shape — loop `overlappedCells(actor)`, check geometry, fire the reaction), scoped
to the same `CARRYABLE_KINDS` list `checkPhysicsObjectSwitchContacts` already uses (pushable, Large
Stone, every carried-tile kind) rather than Plate's own wider "any actor" scope — a wandering NPC
stumbling onto a goal tile by its own steering was never the ask, this toy's "guide blocks through
the machine" premise was. Wired into `stepActor`'s existing `if (def && CARRYABLE_KINDS.includes(...))`
branch right alongside the switch-contact call, and added to `runtimeFunctions` for export. This
level is built consistently with that choice, not just extended-and-forgotten: a spare crate sits
on the spine just before the final Gate, pushable through once it opens, as a real, playable second
way to finish — confirmed end to end (see below) that pushing it into the goal chamber wins the
game exactly like the player's own touch would, in both the live editor and a fresh export.

**Shape, per the brief.** One room (a puzzle box reads best surveyed as a whole, not split across
room transitions — a kid should be able to look at the whole machine and choose an approach), Follow
camera (not Runner — no urgency/fail-state is wanted here), gravity on (cannon/pushable mechanics
are built for the platformer case). `colsForCameraMode` confirms Follow reaches the full
`BOARD_MAX_COLS` (72, 3× the 24-wide viewport) the same as Runner does — plenty of room for three
spatially separate zones plus the final gate/goal.

**Topology, chosen to satisfy "a kid following Route A's path shouldn't stumble onto Route B's
cannon by accident" structurally rather than hoping maze walls alone achieve it.** The SPINE (row
13, walking on a solid floor at row 14) runs the full 72-column width, straight and unobstructed,
from the start to the final Gate — and deliberately carries none of any route's own puzzle content.
Each route is a sealed room ABOVE the spine (rows 1-11, floor at row 12 with exactly one 1-tile gap
as its entrance), reached by jumping straight up through that one gap at a specific column. A kid
who never leaves the spine never encounters any of the three routes at all — confirmed directly in
Phase 2 by walking the spine's full length with zero detours and finding the final Gate still
closed the whole way (see "rule one" below).

**Route A — the direct route** (Zone A, cols 6-27): jump through the entrance gap (col 10), walk a
short corridor, jump over one low pillar (col 16, a deliberately trivial single-tile obstacle — "the
obvious solution for a kid who just starts walking and looking" per the brief, not a challenge),
touch Button A (col 22, shared color). Confirmed by trace: the jump-through-the-entrance step only
works if the player is horizontally aligned with the gap COLUMN before jumping — a first attempt at
this test jumped from an adjacent column and gained zero height at all, bonking its head on the
room's own solid floor one column over; the impulse is wasted entirely if it's not lined up with the
one gap in that floor. Not a bug, just the same "a cell check needs the right cell" lesson every
blocking-geometry gotcha in this file already teaches, re-learned here in test code rather than in
the engine.

**Route B — the indirect machine** (Zone B, cols 30-55): a real Phase 2 finding here, not a guess
that happened to work. The original plan put a PRESSURE PLATE immediately next to its own Gate
doorway, copying what looked like an established "Monster's Den" trick (a plate right beside a
barrier's own cell). Traced directly and found broken: actor width (22px, `ACTOR_SIZE`) is less
than a tile (32px), so there's an unavoidable window, partway across the Gate's own cell, where the
actor has fully left the plate's cell but hasn't yet cleared the Gate's far edge — `plateChannelsActive`
drops the instant overlap ends, and `resolveAxis` reverts the still-mid-gate actor flush back
against the Gate's near edge that same tick. Confirmed by a real frame-by-frame trace: a player held
moving right from the plate oscillates forever at the Gate's boundary, bouncing back to the same
flush position every cycle, never through. Re-reading Monster's Den's own actual mechanism (see that
section above) showed it never relied on this exact transit either — there, something else (a lured
chaser) holds the plate down while the PLAYER walks through separately; nothing in this codebase had
ever validated one actor doing both halves of that handoff itself. Fixed by using a BUTTON (permanent
— once flipped, the channel no longer depends on continued overlap at all) for this LOCAL, zone-internal
reveal instead of a plate, which sidesteps the whole timing question outright. The route's own real
centerpiece — a crate landing on a plate and resting there — is a genuinely different, already-safe
scenario (nothing needs to walk away from a RESTING object in the same motion the way a transiting
actor does), so the plate stays there, unmodified.

Past the now-open local Gate: a cannon (`cannon2-ne`, col 46) with a crate parked immediately
adjacent on its east side (col 47) — grab it (E), face west, throw it in. The exact approach
direction mattered and was traced, not assumed: a cannon fires in a FIXED direction regardless of
which side it was hit from, so an object approaching from the SAME horizontal side the cannon fires
toward gets launched back into the very cell it just left, re-collides with itself on the very next
frame (since the launch velocity carries it further into, not out of, that cell), and has its
velocity zeroed back to 0 by the ordinary "non-bouncy wall" collision resolution before `triggerCannon`'s
own cooldown lets it fire again — confirmed directly: a first version of this test approached
`cannon2-ne` from the west (matching its own fire direction) and the object never actually left the
cannon's cell at all, cannonCooldown staying stuck at 0 forever. Approaching from the OPPOSITE side
(east, moving west, into a `cannon2-ne`) works because the post-fire velocity then points AWAY from
where the object just came from, clearing the cell cleanly on the next frame — confirmed by a
dedicated trace (`trace_cannon_crate.js`) sampling position/velocity every 30ms through the whole
arc. A second real finding from the same trace session: the crate's starting DISTANCE from the
cannon matters more than throw strength might suggest, because friction (`FRICTION_DECEL = 1800`)
decelerates the approach the entire time it's closing the gap — at a 2-tile starting distance, a
bare minimum-charge throw (`THROW_MIN_SPEED = 260`) decays to a dead stop roughly half a tile short
of the cannon and never triggers it at all, confirmed by a batch sweep (`trace_cannon_sweep.js`)
across five throw speeds from two starting distances. Placing the crate directly ADJACENT to the
cannon (the level's own actual layout) closes that gap small enough that every real throw speed from
`THROW_MIN_SPEED` to `THROW_MAX_SPEED` reliably connects (confirmed: all six speeds tested landed
at the exact identical column). Once fired, the post-launch arc is fully deterministic regardless of
approach speed (the cannon overrides velocity outright, per `triggerCannon`) — traced apex at 3
tiles above the cannon's row, landing exactly 3 tiles east of it, every single time (`landed dCol=3.000`
across six separate speed trials) — which is where the second, shared-color plate sits (col 49),
confirmed to trigger (`plateChannelsActive`) the instant the crate settles there, with nobody
needing to hold anything down.

**Route C — the bomb-reveal route** (Zone C, cols 58-66): a bomb (col 63) sits in the open corridor;
arming it (bump into it) and retreating, then waiting out `BOMB_FUSE_MS`, clears both the bomb's own
cell and its one cardinal-adjacent solid neighbor — the wall at col 64, revealing a small pocket
with Button C (shared color). A real, accepted side effect, confirmed by trace rather than treated
as a bug: the bomb's SOUTH cardinal neighbor is the floor itself (row 12 beneath it), which also
clears, leaving a 1-tile gap in the floor directly under where the bomb used to sit — the exact same
"ridiculous contraption" collateral damage the Worst Elevator's own bomb placement already
established as a known, recoverable engine behavior, not something unique to this level. Confirmed
reachable: the revealed space isn't a dead pocket — a simple hop across the collapsed floor gap (the
same trivial single-tile-gap crossing every platformer sample already relies on) reaches the now-open
former-wall cell and the pocket beyond, where Button C flips the shared channel.

**Rule one, verified directly, not assumed from the wall layout alone.** The final Gate is a full
floor-to-ceiling column (rows 1-13, all `barrier-n`, all the shared color) at col 68 — tall enough
that no jump clears it, confirmed by the same walk-the-whole-spine trace mentioned above: holding
right from the start for the time needed to cross all 66 remaining tiles, with zero detours into any
of the three zones, leaves the shared channel off and the player stopped flush against the Gate's own
left edge, not past it. Only once any one of the three routes is completed does the Gate open — confirmed
independently for Route A (push a spare crate through the newly-open Gate, winning via the
object-reaches-goal extension above) and for Routes B/C (their own channel flips, read directly).

**A genuine test-authoring trap, worth recording for the next sample that tests any Play-mode
sequence immediately after `enterPlayMode()`:** the v2.43-era "READY!" pause (`READY_DURATION_MS =
1200`, see that section above) freezes `tick()` entirely for the first 1.2 seconds of every Play
session — a test that sets velocity and samples position within that window will see the exact value
it set, frozen, forever, and can easily misread that as "the physics engine isn't running" or "this
mechanic is broken" rather than "the test needs to wait out the Ready pause first." Hit directly
while testing Zone B's entrance jump (a `vy` set immediately after `enterPlayMode()` stayed pinned,
unintegrated, for a full 500ms of sampling) before the cause was identified and every subsequent test
in this round added an explicit ~1.3s wait after entering Play.

**A second genuine test-authoring trap, worth recording for the same reason:** pushing the spare
crate through the final Gate doesn't just open a path for the player to walk through afterward — the
CRATE reaches the goal tile first (it's ahead of the player, being pushed), and since
`checkPhysicsObjectGoalContacts` fires `triggerGoal` for it exactly like the player's own touch, this
WINS the game outright and freezes the whole simulation (`playSessionEnded = "win"`) well before the
player's own position would ever reach the goal chamber. A first version of this Phase 2 check looked
for the PLAYER's own x-position past the Gate instead, read the resulting freeze (position and
velocity both permanently unchanging) as a stuck blocking-collision bug, and spent real effort
chasing a phantom collision problem before checking `playSessionEnded` directly and finding it was a
confirmed win the whole time, not a stuck state — the exact kind of thing the "object reaches the
goal" extension was built to do, working so well it initially looked like a failure.

**Visual theme**: industrial, deliberately distinct from every pastel/organic sample so far — grey
steel-blue panels with brass rivet accents (`METAL_PANEL_ART`), a darker girder/support-beam texture
for variety (`GIRDER_ART`), and an amber/black diagonal hazard-stripe accent (`HAZARD_STRIPE_ART`)
marking the cannon's own ceiling as "something industrial happens here." Three blueprint-themed Paint
layer backgrounds (`BLUEPRINT_GRID_ART`, `BLUEPRINT_DOT_ART`, `BLUEPRINT_CORNER_ART` — a cross-hatch
grid, a sparse dot grid, and brass corner tick marks on a dark blueprint-blue base) scattered across
the spine, the same "actually use the Paint layer, don't leave it empty" ask Spin Cycle's own section
already addressed, applied again here with a genuinely different palette family.

**No engine code changed beyond the one deliberate extension** (`checkPhysicsObjectGoalContacts`,
covered above, plus its one-line wiring into `stepActor` and `runtimeFunctions`). Every piece used —
Button, Gate, cannon2, bomb2, plate, pushable crates — is an existing, already-validated mechanic;
this level's own contribution is the level content itself plus the one goal-contact extension.

Verified with Playwright across five separate test passes (33 checks total): rule one (the full
spine walk with zero detours, Gate confirmed closed); Route A end to end (entrance jump, pillar
jump, Button, confirmed opening the Gate for the spare-crate win); Route B end to end (entrance
jump, local Button, local Gate, jump over the cannon without self-triggering it, grab, throw,
cannon fire confirmed via `cannonCooldown`, landing column confirmed at the traced col 49); Route C
end to end (bomb arm, retreat, wait out the fuse, wall-clear confirmed cell-by-cell, floor-collapse
side effect confirmed and crossed, Button C confirmed); and a dedicated export test confirming the
object-reaches-goal win fires correctly in a freshly exported standalone game too. The full 7-sample
regression sweep (the six existing samples plus this one) and a fresh Button+Gate export both run
error-free, before and after the debug hook (added fresh for this session, confirmed via `grep -n
"toyDebug" index.html` returning nothing) was stripped.

## "The Gravity Switcheroo" — an eighth sample, and a real new mechanic: the weightlessness pop

A small, single-room physics gag built specifically to showcase one mechanic chain rather than a
sprawling level — the brief itself asked for a lighter verification burden than Catapult Factory,
and the two-phase process (rough layout, then mandatory trace-and-fix) still applied, since this
room chains a dodge, a flip, a diagonal drift, and a ceiling bounce — exactly the "multi-link
trajectory" shape that's repeatedly been the hardest thing to get right by guessing in every prior
sample. `buildGravitySwitcherooGame()`/`drawGravitySwitcherooIcon`, two rooms ("Through the Door," a
short entry hallway, and "The Gravity Switcheroo" itself).

**Naming confirmed before building anything, not assumed**: the gravity-flip tile is an uncolored
`pushbutton` (Button) — `"switch"` was fully retired project-wide in v2.37 (folded into Button and
removed). `triggerFlip`'s existing uncolored branch already flips the room's own `gravityEnabled`
hot global; nothing new was needed there beyond the pop below.

**The weightlessness pop — genuinely new behavior, called out as such rather than discovered.**
Standing still means zero vertical velocity; simply switching a downward force off doesn't create
upward motion from nothing — left alone, the flip would read as "suddenly frozen in place," not
"weightless." `GRAVITY_OFF_POP_SPEED` (420) is a one-time additive `vy -=` applied to **every actor
in the room** the instant gravity goes OFF (never on the way back on — gravity resuming already
supplies its own downward motion from the first tick). Additive on top of whatever `vy` an actor
already has, the same "preserve existing momentum, don't override it" rule the room's own diagonal
drift already depends on.

**A real bug, caught by tracing before it ever reached a user: reusing `bouncyCooldown` to exempt
the pop from the free-roam speed cap caused unbounded velocity runaway.** The obvious move — every
other real impulse in this file (cannon, bomb-blast, a throw) exempts itself from the ordinary
speed-cap clamp via `bouncyCooldown`/`recentlyLaunched` — was tried first and is exactly wrong here.
That exemption skips the cap *clamp*, but not the *acceleration* `applyAxisFriction` still adds each
frame — safe for a thrown object (never has steering input of its own during the window), but this
room's whole premise is the player holding a run key right through the flip. Confirmed directly: a
trace with the exemption in place and right held through the flip showed `vx` climbing past 800px/s,
the identical "`recentlyLaunched` + held key" runaway this file's own `resolveAxis` comments already
warn about for a different mechanic. Fixed by dropping the exemption entirely — the ordinary
combined-speed clamp still scales both axes by the same factor, which preserves the diagonal ANGLE
(a real "kicked sideways and up" moment) while actually capping the magnitude.

**Two physics facts confirmed by trace, not assumed from the brief's own description, that shaped
the room's whole layout:**
- **This engine has no "coast at reduced speed" state at all**, gravity or free-roam — releasing a
  held key decays velocity to a dead stop within roughly 150-250ms (`FRICTION_DECEL = 1800` is
  strong by design). "Different entry speed into the toggle" can't come from a kid choosing to walk
  in slower; it has to come from a forced direction change — the Chaser's placement is load-bearing
  for this, not just thematic.
- **Free-roam's speed cap is on the combined 2D vector, not per axis** — holding two perpendicular
  directions (up+right) equally settles at `cap/√2 ≈ 155.6` on each of `vx`/`vy`, a true 45° cruise,
  not a faster diagonal than either axis alone could reach. Zero-gravity motion really is the
  simpler case the brief describes once a direction is actually being held — `stepActor`'s free-roam
  branch has no curving force at all, just friction on both axes.

**A real design conflict, found by trace rather than assumed: a single collectible wins the room on
its own, bypassing the whole chain.** `maybeRoundCompleteFromCollectibles` (project-wide,
unconditional — "goal and full collection both funnel through here, one completion reaction, two
ways to reach it") fires the instant the last live collectible is gathered. With only the one gem
the brief describes near the spike ring, grabbing it — which happens naturally while just walking
